      "heading": "How to Choose a QuickBooks Alternative",
      "body": "Start with the billing unit, because it decides how the price grows. QuickBooks charges by plan tier, and each tier caps the number of users: one on Simple Start, three on Essentials, five on Plus and 25 on Advanced. FreshBooks charges by billable clients, so few clients with many invoices cost little, while hundreds of small clients move you to the top plan. Xero and Zoho Books charge by tier, and Zoho Books also publishes user and invoice limits on every tier. Wave charges nothing for software on Starter and earns its fee from card payments.\n\nNext, count the people who need a login. A two-person firm fits QuickBooks Essentials, Zoho Books Standard, or a FreshBooks plan plus a paid team-member seat. Check whether your bookkeeper or accountant is counted as a user or has a separate free seat, since QuickBooks lists two accountant seats on its lower plans and Zoho Books includes one accountant on its free plan.\n\nThen check what the entry plan limits. Xero Early caps invoices and quotes at 20 and bills at 5. QuickBooks Free allows 2 invoices a month. Zoho Invoice allows 500 invoices a year. A cap that looks generous at 10 clients can bind at 100.\n\nFinally, separate subscription prices from payment fees and add-ons. Card processing, Automated Clearing House (ACH) bank transfers, payroll and extra users are priced apart from the monthly plan. Wave payroll, FreshBooks payroll at $40 a month plus $6 per user, and Xero payroll at $36 a month plus $6 per person all sit on top of the plan.\n\nA short checklist:\n\n- How many people need to log in, and is the accountant counted?\n- How many invoices, bills and clients do you handle in a busy month?\n- Do you need inventory, project profitability or payroll, and what does each cost?\n- What do card and bank payments cost on top of the plan?\n- Can your accountant work in the product, or will they ask for an export?\n\nPromotional prices last 3 or 4 months and then revert, so compare regular prices. For a longer view of the category, the [invoicing apps roundup](/roundup/best-invoicing-apps-for-freelancers/) lists more tools priced by the same units."

    "chooseSection": {
      "heading": "How to Choose a Quicken Alternative",
      "body": "Choosing between Quicken alternatives usually comes down to five questions: how the app charges, which devices it runs on, how it treats budgeting, what it does with investments and whether the provider publishes a price at all. The criteria below are checks to run on any provider, not a ranking.\n\n**Billing structure.** Monarch and YNAB both list $14.99 a month, but their annual prices differ: $99.99 for Monarch and $109 for YNAB. Copilot lists $9 a month and $109 a year, so its yearly option costs about one dollar more than twelve monthly payments. Quicken Simplifi lists a lower entry price for the first year, and renewal happens at the then-current price. Compare the renewal price, not only the first-year offer.\n\n**Platform.** Quicken Classic keeps data on a Windows or Mac computer. Simplifi, Monarch and YNAB run on the web and mobile. Copilot lists iPhone, iPad, Mac and web, so it does not cover Android or Windows apps. Quicken also states its products are not designed to work outside the US and Canada.\n\n**Budgeting method.** YNAB is built around assigning every dollar a job, often called zero-based budgeting. Monarch and Copilot lean toward syncing accounts into a dashboard with categories and goals. Readers who want to test a method before paying can try the site's [zero-based budget calculator](/budget/zero-based-budget-calculator/) and read the [guide to making a budget](/guides/how-to-make-a-budget/).\n\n**Investments and net worth.** Empower is built around a net worth and investment dashboard. Quicken lists investment tracking in some plans, and Monarch lists deeper investment features in its Plus tier.\n\n**Price transparency.** Empower and Rocket Money do not publish a comparable single price on the pages checked, so a 12-month cost cannot be calculated for them without guessing.\n\n- Check whether the price is monthly or billed annually.\n- Check the renewal price after any first-year offer.\n- Check platform support, including Android and desktop.\n- Check which institutions and account types connect."

    ],
    "stillBetterFit": {
      "heading": "When Quicken Is Still the Better Fit",
      "body": "Quicken remains the closer match for people who already keep years of records in Quicken Classic, since moving history to another app can mean starting a new record. It also fits people who prefer desktop software with data stored locally on a Windows or Mac computer, which none of the alternatives above offer.\n\nSimplifi lists a lower entry price than Monarch, YNAB or Copilot in the first year, and Quicken lists investment tracking, a retirement planner and cash-flow projections in its plans. Several alternatives are web or mobile only. For readers who want a Quicken-style product, the listed first-year offer ends for new memberships ordered after December 13, 2026, and renewal happens at the then-current price.\n\nQuicken also keeps a two-line structure, cloud Simplifi and desktop Classic, so readers can choose by storage preference. Compare the renewal price with the alternatives before relying on the first-year figure. For related context, the [compare hub](/compare/) lists other product comparisons."
    },
    "verdict": "Among Quicken alternatives, Monarch, YNAB and Copilot publish subscription prices in a similar range, from $99.99 to $109 a year or $9 to $14.99 a month, while Quicken Simplifi lists a lower first-year entry price. Empower and Rocket Money do not publish a single comparable price on the pages checked. Which product fits depends on platform, budgeting method and billing preference, and the figures can change.       "body": "The cost of an international transfer has two parts: the fee the provider charges, and the gap between the exchange rate it applies and the mid-market rate, which is the midpoint between buy and sell prices on currency markets.     "intro": "TaxAct, 1040.com, Online Taxes (OLT), Jackson Hewitt Online, ezTaxReturn and Keeper are six TurboTax alternatives with published filing prices. 1040.com and Jackson Hewitt Online charge flat rates, OLT offers $0 all-forms federal filing, TaxAct and ezTaxReturn separate service tiers, and Keeper pairs year-round bookkeeping with filing. At The Modern Wallet, we compare published tax-filing fees and show how state returns, form upgrades and payment choices change the bill.\n\n[TurboTax](https://turbotax.intuit.com/personal-taxes/online/) Online lists do-it-yourself filing at $0 to $139 federal, Expert Assist at $59 to $209 federal, and Expert Full Service starting at $129 federal. Paid returns have additional state fees, and Intuit states a $40 service fee may apply when paying individual online fees from a federal or state refund.     "chooseSection": {
      "heading": "How to Choose a TurboTax Alternative",
      "body": "List the forms you need before comparing federal prices. TurboTax Free Edition excludes Schedule A itemized deductions, Schedule C self-employment income, rental property and investment sales. OLT includes those forms in its $0 federal tier, while 1040.com includes supported forms in its $34.99 flat price. If stock sales are on your list, the [stock capital gains tax calculator](/stock-capital-gains-tax-calculator/) can help you work through the gain separately from filing fees.\n\nCount state returns next. OLT charges per state, while 1040.com and Jackson Hewitt Online include multiple states in one price. TurboTax and TaxAct say state filing costs extra on paid returns but do not give a fixed state dollar figure on their checked overview pages. A second state therefore changes the published OLT total but leaves those two paid-plan totals unknown.\n\nDecide whether you will prepare the return yourself, ask for expert review, or hand it to a preparer. Those are different services at TaxAct and TurboTax. Paying filing fees from a refund may add a $40 service fee at TurboTax Online or $39.95 at ezTaxReturn. For quarterly-payment math outside the filing software bill, use the [self-employment tax calculator](/self-employment-tax/).\n\n- Which schedules do you need, including Schedule A or Schedule C?\n- How many state returns will you file?\n- Do you need live advice, a final review or full-service preparation?\n- Will you pay filing fees directly or from a federal refund?\n- Would year-round expense tracking be useful, or do you only need filing?"

      {
        "heading": "TaxAct Pricing, Plans and Limits",
        "body": "TaxAct offers self-filing, expert help and full-service preparation, with state fees additional on each path. Do It Yourself starts at $0 federal. TaxAct Free Edition covers simple Form 1040 returns without attached forms or schedules, and TaxAct says about 40% of users qualify. Deluxe, Premier and Self-Employed are the named paid self-filing tiers, but the checked online page does not itemize their upper prices or per-state dollar fees.\n\nTaxAct's expert-help prices need a closer read because the same page gives two starting figures. Its service description says Xpert Assist starts at $45 federal, while its comparison table says $54. TaxAct also lists standalone Xpert Assist for $60 on its consumer Form 1040 products. Unlimited live chat and phone help plus a quick expert review let you prepare the return yourself while getting help before filing.\n\nXpert Full Service starts at $99 federal, plus state fees. A dedicated credentialed tax expert prepares and files the return through video and phone consultations, so this path serves a different job from self-filing. TaxAct also lists a $100,000 Accuracy Guarantee for qualifying software calculation errors, a Refund Transfer payment option, E-file Concierge phone status notifications and optional three-year Audit Defense. The checked page does not give dollar fees for those optional services.\n\nTaxAct fits filers who want to choose how much preparation work to do themselves. For a straightforward W-2 and Form 1040 return, Xpert Full Service starts $30 below TurboTax Expert Full Service's $129 federal starting price: $129 minus $99 equals $30. Both charge additional state fees, so that calculation compares federal starting prices only. TaxAct Free Edition also does not match TurboTax Free Edition's published $0 state filing for qualifying simple returns."
      },
      {
        "heading": "1040.com Flat-Rate Federal and Multi-State Pricing",
        "body": "1040.com publishes a $34.99 flat price for federal and state returns together, including multiple states. You pay when you file. The price covers supported forms and schedules at the same rate whether the return has W-2 wages or more complex income. There is no $0 tier for a simple Form 1040, so a qualifying TurboTax Free Edition filer would pay $34.99 instead of $0.\n\nThe included forms cover Schedule C contractor and small-business income, Health Savings Account forms, charitable donations and home ownership or home sale. Student tax situations, retirement income, unemployment, investments, stock and cryptocurrency sales, and rental property are also listed. Those inclusions matter when a return needs forms outside TurboTax Free Edition: 1040.com does not raise its published flat price for those listed forms. Filing an April 15 extension to submit by October 15 is included as well.\n\n1040.com operates as an Internal Revenue Service (IRS)-authorized electronic filing provider under Taxwell, and you enter return details before paying anything at filing. That pricing rule keeps the math simple when comparing a multi-form return against tiered software. For a freelancer with Schedule C income, stock sales and two state returns, homepage pricing is $34.99 plus $0 for those forms and $0 for the states, or $34.99 total.\n\n1040.com fits filers who want a known combined price, particularly after a move requiring two state returns. TurboTax requires a paid Do It Yourself tier for Schedule C and stock sales, with federal filing priced up to $139 and states additional. Unlike TurboTax Expert Assist and Expert Full Service, 1040.com's checked homepage describes self-guided filing and knowledge-base support rather than a dedicated preparer or live screen-sharing tier."

        "body": "Online Taxes (OLT) includes simple and complex federal returns in its $0 Federal Free Edition. A state return costs $9.95 on that plan. OLT's Premium Edition costs $7.95 federal plus $7.95 per state, a $2 reduction in the regular state price. Both tiers include all forms, so moving to Premium pays for support and other services rather than adding Schedule C or investment forms.\n\nFederal Free Edition lists mortgage interest and property-tax deductions, cryptocurrency and stock sales, dividends, capital gains, rental income and depreciation. Prior-year data import and Artificial Intelligence (AI) W-2 and 1099 import are listed, alongside email support. This gives a filer with Schedule C or stock sales a published $0 federal price, while TurboTax Free Edition requires a paid upgrade for those forms.\n\nPremium adds phone and live chat support, audit support, year-over-year return comparison and priority processing. Those features give you another way to ask filing questions and compare returns without changing OLT's form coverage. With one state, Federal Free Edition costs $0 plus $9.95, or $9.95 total. Premium costs $7.95 plus $7.95, or $15.90 total, making the support upgrade $5.95 for that one-state return.\n\nOLT fits self-employed filers and investors willing to prepare their own returns. Investors deciding how much to contribute to a Roth IRA can run numbers in the [Roth IRA calculator](/investing/roth-ira-calculator/) before comparing account fees.\n\nPublished commissions cannot measure execution quality or lock in variable cash APYs. Where a brokerage omits a margin tier or cash sweep yield from its main fee overview, leave that line unpriced rather than plugging an estimate into a 12-month total."
      },
      {
        "heading": "What Would Change Our Answer on Robinhood Alternatives",
        "body": "A change in the published fee schedule can reverse a 12-month cost comparison. The $20,000 cash example ties only while Robinhood Gold costs $60 yearly and the listed cash APYs remain 3.6% and 3.30%. Five changes would call for new arithmetic:\n\n- A change in Robinhood Gold's $5 monthly price or its 3% IRA match and holding rules.\n- A move in Robinhood's or Public's cash APY that changes the $20,000 cash break-even point.\n- A change in Public's $0.06 to $0.18 options rebate terms or a trader's eligibility for them.\n- A change in the contract volume needed for E*TRADE's $0.50 options tier or Interactive Brokers' lower futures tier.\n- A change in a published margin rate, futures commission or $75 outgoing transfer fee.\n\nRobinhood Gold's cash yield is an eligible brokerage-cash rate, while E*TRADE's listed 4.25% is a six-month promotional savings rate. Treating those as the same kind of ongoing rate would change the comparison without any brokerage changing its price. Recalculate with the rates and contract counts that apply to the account you are considering."
      }

Standard Robinhood accounts charge $0 equity-options commissions plus the $0.04 combined per-contract fee.\n\nIf you already hold a brokerage account elsewhere, include outgoing transfer fees before switching: both E*TRADE and Webull list $75 outgoing transfer charges. Shared $0 stock commissions alone do not justify moving accounts."
    },

    "chooseSection": {
      "heading": "How to Choose a Rocket Money Alternative",
      "body": "Start with the job Rocket Money is doing. Its Free tier lists every recurring subscription, and the app contacts providers to cancel for Premium members. None of the alternatives below advertises hands-off cancellation on the pages checked, so a household that mainly wants someone else to cancel services is weighing a feature as well as a price.\n\nNext, separate budgeting depth from subscription handling. Monarch, YNAB, Copilot Money, Simplifi and PocketGuard each emphasise budgets, cash flow or spending plans, while Honeydue emphasises sharing between two people. Rocket Money's free tier caps custom category budgets at two, so anyone who wants more than two categories has to compare the Premium tier against these apps.\n\nThen compare billing units. Monarch, YNAB, Copilot Money and PocketGuard publish yearly prices that are lower than twelve months at the monthly rate, and PocketGuard adds a lifetime option. Simplifi's lower figure is a first-year offer, so the second-year figure matters.\n\nRun through these five checks before picking an app:\n\n- Does the household need cancellation done for it, or only a list of subscriptions?\n- How many budget categories are needed, and does a free tier cap them?\n- Is the app used by one person, a couple or a larger group?\n- Which devices are in use: iPhone, Android, Mac or web?\n- Is a yearly or one-time payment acceptable, or does monthly billing matter?\n\nFor a variable paycheck, the guide on [budgeting with irregular income](/guides/how-to-budget-with-irregular-income/) explains why a fixed monthly plan can break, and it applies to every app on this page."
    },
    "stillBetterFit": {
      "heading": "When Rocket Money Is Still the Better Fit",
      "body": "Rocket Money fits households whose first problem is recurring charges they cannot see or stop. Its Free tier finds and lists subscriptions, and Premium members can have Rocket Money contact providers to cancel. Premium+ adds bill negotiation for services such as cable, internet, cell phone plans and home security, which none of the alternatives above lists on the pages checked.\n\nIts free tier also costs $0 for spending tracking, bill tracking and two custom category budgets. Premium covers credit reports with weekly updates to a Fair Isaac Corporation (FICO) Score 2 from Experian, financial goals that move small amounts into a Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation (FDIC) insured account, and account sharing.\n\nFor someone who would pay $15 per month for Premium+ mainly to have a team negotiate bills, a $99.99 or $109 yearly budgeting app does not replace that service. The comparison depends on whether a saved bill covers the subscription fee, so the comparison rests on current monthly bills and the services that need handling."
    },

      {
        "heading": "Monarch Pricing, Plans and Household Features",
        "body": "Monarch is a subscription-funded budgeting and net worth app. Its pricing page lists $99.99 per year or $14.99 per month, a first week free and a money-back guarantee. A Plus tier adds forecasting tools, tracking for multiple income sources such as a small business and deeper investment tracking, though the pricing page checked does not list a Plus price.\n\nMonarch also publishes a household concept, so transactions can be flagged as needing review and assigned to a family member. It recommends signing up on the web rather than through the Apple or Google app stores for easier subscription management.\n\nThe yearly math is simple. Twelve months at $14.99 costs $179.88, while the yearly plan costs $99.99, a $79.89 difference. Compared with Rocket Money Premium+ at $15 per month, which is $180 over twelve months, Monarch's yearly plan costs $80.01 less, though the two apps include different services.\n\nMonarch falls short of Rocket Money on the pages checked in one respect: it does not advertise cancelling subscriptions or negotiating bills on a member's behalf.     "intro": "Trust & Will, Rocket Lawyer, FreeWill, ZenBusiness, Northwest Registered Agent and Bizee are six LegalZoom alternatives, covering online wills and living trusts, attorney memberships and business formation. Its living trusts start at $399 for an individual, and a limited liability company (LLC) starts at $0 plus state filing fees, rising to $249 for Pro and $299 for Premium.     "chooseSection": {
      "heading": "How to Choose a LegalZoom Alternative",
      "body": "Start by separating the personal side from the business side. Trust & Will and FreeWill cover wills and estate documents, Rocket Lawyer sells legal documents and attorney access by membership, and ZenBusiness, Northwest and Bizee focus on forming a business. LegalZoom sells all three categories, which is why a like-for-like comparison needs the right column of the table.\n\nNext, compare what the headline price includes. LegalZoom's Basic Will at $129 lists a will, pet care provisions, online storage, 30 days of revisions and one printed set. Its Pro Will at $149 adds a healthcare directive, medical and financial powers of attorney, a Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act (HIPAA) authorization and 30 days of attorney consultations. Trust & Will's $199 Will Plan lists a will, a HIPAA authorization, a living will and a power of attorney in a single price.\n\nThen add the renewal terms. LegalZoom's Pro Will consultations renew at $25 per month, and its Premium Will and Trust consultations renew at $199 per year. Its LLC Pro and Premium tiers renew at $49 per month for attorney consultations and $9.99 per month for bookkeeping tools. Rocket Lawyer's pricing is a recurring membership from the start.\n\nRun through these checks before comparing totals:\n\n- Is the need a will, a living trust, an LLC or several of them?\n- Does the plan price cover one person or a couple?\n- Are attorney consultations included, optional or a recurring charge?\n- Are state filing fees, a registered agent or an EIN priced separately?\n- Does the first-year price differ from the renewal price?\n\nFor the cost of the documents themselves, the [living trust cost calculator](/estate-planning/living-trust-cost-calculator/) and the [probate versus trust comparison](/compare/probate-vs-trust/) show what a trust adds.         "heading": "Bizee Formation Packages and Free Registered Agent Year",
        "body": "Bizee, the service formerly branded Incfile, lists three one-time formation packages: Basic at $0, Standard at $199 and Premium at $299, each plus the state fee. Its pricing page says each package is eligible for one free year of registered agent service. The Basic package carries the lowest price for the core filing, and the higher tiers add services that Bizee lists on its package comparison.\n\nA worked example with an illustrative $100 state fee puts Bizee Basic at $100, Standard at $299 and Premium at $399. LegalZoom's LLC tiers sit at $0, $249 and $299 before the same state fee, so on the headline numbers Bizee Standard costs $50 less than LegalZoom Pro and Bizee Premium and LegalZoom Premium both list $299 before state fees. LegalZoom's Pro tier lists an operating agreement, an EIN, 30 days of business attorney consultations and a year of document access, so the package contents differ and a column-by-column check matters more than the headline price.\n\nBizee falls short of LegalZoom for people who also want a will or trust, because it sells business services only. It fits business owners who want one-time package pricing and a first year of registered agent service included."
      },
      {
        "heading": "Who This Is Not For",
        "body": "Several needs sit outside what these providers list:\n\n- Estates with complex family or tax facts, such as special needs, blended families or high-net-worth estates; Trust & Will points these to attorney-led plans starting at $1,499, and FreeWill's page tells people with complex needs to seek legal counsel.\n- Readers who need a living trust outside California without paying; FreeWill offers its trust in California only.\n- Trademark, patent or copyright work, which none of the three formation services above lists on the pages checked; LegalZoom lists those separately.\n- Anyone needing legal advice on a specific situation; these pages list products and published prices only. Rocket Lawyer separates legal information from advice and sells a Legal Advice Session at $149 for 30 minutes.\n\nTo size a plan before shopping, the [first-time estate planning guide](/guides/first-time-estate-planning/) lists the documents most households start with."
      },
      {
        "heading": "What Would Change Our Answer on LegalZoom Alternatives",
        "body": "A published price change could shift the lowest-cost route for the same documents:\n\n- LegalZoom ending its up-to-20%-off holiday sale on December 13, which returns the Basic Will to $129 and the Premium Will to $299.\n- Trust & Will publishing a couple price for its Will Plan or Trust Plan, or changing the $299 attorney support add-on.\n- Rocket Lawyer changing its $149, $249 or $349 yearly membership prices or its $99 charge for later registrations.\n- FreeWill adding a fee or extending its living trust beyond California.\n- ZenBusiness, Northwest or Bizee changing a $0, $39 or $199 formation price, or moving registered agent service in or out of a package.\n- Nolo's WillMaker pages becoming readable, which would add another will-software row to the table.\n\nBrowse the [financial product comparison hub](/compare/) for other fee breakdowns."
      }
    ],
    "stillBetterFit": {
      "heading": "When LegalZoom Is Still the Better Fit",
      "body": "LegalZoom covers both personal and business paperwork under one account. A household that needs a will and also plans to form an LLC can buy both there, with the same login, the same document storage and the same attorney network. Rocket Lawyer is the one alternative above that bundles documents and a first LLC registration, and it does so as a membership rather than as separately priced wills and trusts.\n\nLegalZoom's tiers are also priced to bundle attorney access. The Pro Will adds 30 days of attorney consultations for $20 more than the $129 Basic Will, and the Premium Will adds a year of consultations, annual estate planning review and 10% off LegalZoom products. Its LLC Pro tier lists an operating agreement, an EIN and unlimited 30-minute attorney consultations for 30 days in one $249 package, while ZenBusiness prices the operating agreement and EIN at $99 each.\n\nThe holiday sale is a third factor. LegalZoom lists up to 20% off a will or trust through December 13, with a Basic Will at $116.10 and a Premium Will at $239.20 on the sale page, compared with the $129 and $299 list prices. A sale that ends can narrow or close a gap with a flat-priced competitor."
    },

LegalZoom lists priced wills, living trusts and LLC formation as separate products on one site, while Rocket Lawyer bundles documents and LLC registration in a membership.\n\nTo compare LegalZoom alternatives for your own situation, write down whether you need a will, a trust or an LLC, whether the plan covers one person or two, and whether attorney consultations or a registered agent are needed.     "intro": "Wave, Zoho Invoice, Zoho Books, Xero, Invoice Ninja, Bonsai and Square Invoices are seven FreshBooks alternatives, with entry prices that run from $0 plans to Xero's $27 per month Early plan. Credit card payments through FreshBooks Payments cost 2.9% + $0.30 per transaction, and Automated Clearing House (ACH) bank transfers cost 1%. Add-ons are priced separately: Team Members $11 per month per user, Advanced Payments $20 per month, and FreshBooks Payroll $40 per month plus $6 per month per user.\n\nQuickBooks has its own page, covered in the [QuickBooks alternatives comparison](/alternatives/quickbooks/), and a price-by-users breakdown sits in the [QuickBooks vs FreshBooks comparison](/compare/quickbooks-vs-freshbooks/).     "chooseSection": {
      "heading": "How to Choose a FreshBooks Alternative",
      "body": "Start with the client count, because it sets the FreshBooks plan. Lite covers 5 billable clients, Plus covers 50 and Premium is unlimited. A freelancer with 12 clients lands on Plus at $43 per month, or $516 per year, while Wave Starter, Zoho Invoice and Invoice Ninja Pro list lower subscription prices for the same 12 clients.\n\nNext, separate the subscription from the payment fees. FreshBooks, Wave, Square and the other tools that process payments each charge a percentage per card payment on top of the plan price. On a $2,000 invoice paid by card, 2.9% + $0.30 comes to $58.30, and 3.3% + $0.30 comes to $66.30. Bank transfers cost less: 1% of $2,000 is $20.\n\nThen match the tool to the kind of record needed. Invoicing tools produce bills and track what clients owe. Accounting tools such as Zoho Books and Xero keep a full ledger with bank reconciliation. Bonsai sits closer to client management, with proposals, contracts and scheduling. For the ledger side, the [accounting software for freelancers](/roundup/best-accounting-software-for-freelancers/) roundup covers the bookkeeping options in more depth.\n\nRun through these checks before comparing prices:\n\n- How many clients are billed in a year, and does the count include archived clients?\n- Will clients pay by card, bank transfer or both, and who absorbs the processing fee?\n- Is a full ledger with bank reconciliation needed, or only invoices?\n- How many people need a login, and is the seat price per user?\n- Is payroll, mileage tracking or proposal software already paid for elsewhere? The [mileage tracker apps](/roundup/best-mileage-tracker-apps/) roundup lists dedicated options."

    "metaDescription": "Compare Monarch Money alternatives: YNAB, Copilot Money, Quicken Simplifi, PocketGuard, Tiller and Goodbudget, with regular plan prices and trials.",
    "published": "2026-11-17",
    "updated": "2026-11-17",
    "intro": "YNAB, Copilot Money, Quicken Simplifi, PocketGuard, Tiller and Goodbudget are six Monarch Money alternatives, with regular entry prices ranging from a $0 free plan to $109 per year. [Monarch Money](https://www.monarchmoney.com/pricing) sells two subscription tiers: Core at $99.99 per year or $14.99 per month, and Plus at $199.99 per year. Monarch Money lists a free first week under the phrase \"Your first week is on us\" and provides a no-questions-asked money-back guarantee with permission to cancel anytime. Monarch Money notes that it displays no ads, states that it never sells financial data, and maintains a 4.9 rating across more than 100,000 reviews as stated on its pricing page. Monarch Money recommends completing sign-up on the web rather than through the Apple App Store or Google Play.\n\nFor readers who want a broader overview of personal finance software, our [best budgeting apps roundup](/roundup/best-budgeting-apps/) tracks wider category options. Partners managing money together can examine the [best budgeting apps for couples roundup](/roundup/best-budgeting-apps-for-couples/), and planners establishing spending categories can review our [guide on how to make a budget](/guides/how-to-make-a-budget/).",

    ],

    "chooseSection": {
      "heading": "How to Choose a Monarch Money Alternative",
      "body": "Selecting a replacement for Monarch Money begins with five operational factors: the billing commitment, the length of the evaluation trial, the method of connecting financial accounts, the underlying budgeting model, and whether an application interface or a spreadsheet fits daily tracking habits.\n\nFirst, assess the billing commitment. Monarch Money Core charges $99.99 on an annual basis or $14.99 on a month-to-month schedule. Paying month to month for 12 months totals $179.88, which is an $79.89 premium over the single annual payment. YNAB charges $109 annually or $14.99 monthly, yielding the same $179.88 annual total on monthly billing. PocketGuard offers an annual tier at $74.99, a monthly rate at $12.99 (which totals $155.88 over 12 months), and a permanent Lifetime purchase at $149.99. Tiller avoids monthly subscriptions entirely by charging $99 per year. Goodbudget provides a $0 tier alongside an $80 annual plan or a $10 monthly rate ($120 per year).\n\nSecond, evaluate the free trial terms. Monarch Money lists a 7-day free trial under the phrase \"Your first week is on us.\" PocketGuard also uses a 7-day trial across its plans. In contrast, YNAB offers a 34-day free trial that requires no credit card details at registration, meaning no charge occurs unless the user chooses to subscribe. Tiller grants a 30-day trial without charging the card until the period concludes, paired with a subsequent 30-day refund window that offers up to 60 days of risk-free evaluation. Copilot Money and Quicken Simplifi list no specific free trial days on their pricing pages.\n\nThird, review bank connectivity and platforms. Quicken Simplifi connects to 14,000+ financial institutions, while Copilot Money connects to 16,000+ banks. Tiller pulls transaction data automatically into personal spreadsheets. Goodbudget restricts automatic bank synchronization to United States (US) banks on its Premium tier. Copilot Money supports iPhone, iPad, Mac, and Web, whereas Goodbudget supports Web, Android, and iPhone.\n\nFourth, determine the budgeting methodology. Monarch Money offers general expense categorizing, forecasting, and investment monitoring. Users can test zero-allocation math using our [zero-based budget calculator](/budget/zero-based-budget-calculator/). Quicken Simplifi organizes spending through a custom Spending Plan and projects cash flow 12 months into the future.       "heading": "How to Choose a Mercury Alternative",
      "body": "Selecting an alternative to Mercury requires evaluating fee structures, interest requirements, branch access, multi-currency handling, and team expense controls.\n\nFirst, assess transaction and wire charges. Wise Business charges a fixed $6.11 fee to receive domestic wires and sends wire transfers starting at $1.13. Chase Business Complete Banking charges a $15 monthly service fee (unless waived) and limits teller transactions, while Performance Business Checking at $40 per month includes incoming wires and your two most expensive outgoing domestic wires.\n\nSecond, compare yields on idle cash balances. Mercury Treasury offers up to a 4.42% yield through the JPMorgan U.S. Treasury Plus Money Market Fund, but restricts eligibility to accounts holding $250,000 or more. If your balance sits below that threshold, Relay provides 1.19% APY on Starter ($0 per month), 1.87% APY on Grow ($30 per month), and 3.21% APY on Scale ($90 per month). Bluevine Standard offers 1.3% APY if you meet a monthly activity goal, Plus offers 1.75% APY up to $250,000 for $30 per month, and Premier offers 3.3% APY for $95 per month. Lili offers variable savings yields of 2.25% APY up to $500,000 and 4.00% APY from $500,000 to $1,000,000, while Novo Savings requires a minimum balance of $50,000 to earn 2% APY.\n\nThird, verify account structure and team delegation. Relay allows up to 20 individual checking accounts on Starter and up to 50 on Scale, with 50 debit and credit cards across all tiers. Brex organizes corporate spend across teams, charging $0 per user on Essentials and $12 per user per month on Premium for custom expense review chains and live budgets. Mercury includes virtual and physical cards with spend limits and duplicate subscription detection.\n\nRun through these five practical checks before switching providers:\n\n- Does the business deposit physical cash or require teller services at a branch?\n- Does the company send domestic or foreign currency wires every month?\n- What is your average operating balance, and does it meet minimum interest thresholds?\n- How many distinct checking accounts does your bookkeeping system require?\n- How many employees need corporate cards or custom payment approvals?"

Among alternatives, Relay pays 1.19% APY on savings in Starter, 1.87% APY in Grow ($30 per month), and 3.21% APY in Scale ($90 per month). Lili pays 2.25% APY on savings balances up to $500,000 and 4.00% APY on balances from $500,000 up to $1,000,000 across all tiers. Novo Savings pays 2% APY at balances of $50,000 or more. Mercury provides a Treasury account for balances of $250,000 or more, while Chase business checking accounts do not advertise base interest yields.\n\nThird, verify FDIC insurance coverage limits. Standard FDIC insurance protects up to $250,000 per depositor per institution. Bluevine, Relay and Lili extend FDIC protection up to $3,000,000 by distributing funds across partner sweep networks. Mercury and Novo provide sweep coverage protecting deposits up to $5,000,000. \n\nFourth, assess transaction charges and physical branch access. Bluevine includes free incoming domestic wires and free standard ACH payments, but applies charges for outgoing wires and international payments. Mercury provides free incoming and outgoing ACH transfers, domestic wires, international wires and real-time payments across all plans. Chase provides physical branch teller transactions and fee-free cash deposits up to $5,000 per cycle on Business Complete Banking, but restricts fee-free wire allowances to higher-tier checking accounts. \n\nFifth, review credit availability. Bluevine offers an embedded business line of credit up to $250,000 with no monthly maintenance fees and automatic weekly or monthly repayments. Lili embeds flexible capital up to $250,000. OnDeck operates as a dedicated commercial financing lender, providing revolving lines of credit from $6,000 to $200,000 with an average published rate of 59.8% Annual Percentage Rate (APR), and term loans up to $400,000 with an average rate of 53.2% APR. Mercury, Relay and Novo do not list a line of credit on their pricing pages, though Novo offers a business credit card with up to 2% cash back and Relay offers credit cards with cash back scaling from 1% to 1.5%.\n\nSixth, check business formation and entity restrictions. Bluevine requires a business to be structured as a corporation or Limited Liability Company (LLC) to qualify for its line of credit, excluding sole proprietorships from borrowing. OnDeck requires an operational business checking account, one year in business, and $100,000 in annual revenue. Lili limits international wire transfers exclusively to LLCs, corporations and partnerships.\n\nWho This Is Not For:\n\n- Companies in Nevada, North Dakota, South Dakota, or United States territories, which cannot obtain a Bluevine Line of Credit.\n- Sole proprietors, because the Bluevine Line of Credit requires an LLC or corporation.\n- Businesses that need branch cash deposits beyond what Chase offers.\n- Personal finance readers, because this page covers business accounts only.\n\nWhat Would Change Our Answer:\n\n- Bluevine altering its 1.3%, 1.75% or 3.3% checking APY rates or removing its debit spend and deposit activity requirements on Standard.\n- Relay, Mercury or Novo introducing an integrated revolving commercial line of credit to rival Bluevine.\n- Chase removing its $15 monthly fee on Business Complete Banking or eliminating branch deposit allowances.\n- OnDeck lowering its $100,000 revenue qualification minimum or publishing individual risk-tier rates below its 53.2% and 59.8% APR averages.\n- Lili publishing specific monthly plan prices for its Pro, Smart and Premium accounts."

    "chooseSection": {
      "heading": "How to Choose an Empower Alternative",
      "body": "Start by identifying the primary job you want the software to do. Empower pairs free tracking tools with a wealth management advisory service. If your goal is to have someone manage your investments, automated investment managers like Betterment, Wealthfront and Fidelity Go manage portfolios for a stated fee.\n\nNext, evaluate how each service charges for its tools. In contrast, Monarch Money charges $99.99 per year for Core or $199.99 for Plus, YNAB charges $109 per year, and Quicken Simplifi charges $3.99 per month billed annually ($47.88 per year) or a listed $6.99 per month ($83.88 per year). When choosing tracking tools, decide whether you prefer a free dashboard or a paid subscription.\n\nFor investment advisory services, compare balance minimums and fee structures. Empower requires $100,000 for its Personal Strategy tier. That tier provides a dedicated financial advisor, custom portfolio construction, and tax optimization. Private Client requires $1,000,000. Below $100,000, Empower wealth management is not available. Betterment charges $5 a month, or 0.25% annually with $24,000 or $200 monthly deposits. Its Premium tier needs $100,000 and costs 0.65% annually. Wealthfront charges 0.25% a year. Fidelity Go charges a $0 advisory fee for accounts under $25,000, and 0.35% annually for accounts of $25,000 or more with one-on-one coaching included.\n\nReview the cash management terms if cash yield matters to you. Empower Personal Cash provides a standard 3.50% APY with up to $5 million in aggregate FDIC insurance and no minimums or account fees, with a 3.70% APY available for eligible Private Client members. Betterment Cash Reserve lists a base 3.50% APY. A 0.75% boost applies to new clients with qualifying deposits up to $1,000,000. Joint FDIC insurance reaches $8 million.\n\nUse this checklist to narrow your choice:\n\n- Do you need software to track your spending and net worth, or do you need an investment manager to handle trades?\n- Do you meet Empower's $100,000 minimum for Personal Strategy, or do you prefer an advisory service with lower thresholds?\n- Would you rather pay an annual subscription for an ad-free budgeting app, or use Empower's free dashboard?\n- Do you require direct access to a human financial advisor, or does automated portfolio rebalancing meet your requirements?\n- What interest rate does your uninvested cash earn, and what is the applicable FDIC coverage limit?"

Joint FDIC insurance reaches $8 million. Betterment also supports Individual Retirement Account (IRA) transfers.\n\nBetterment fits investors who want automated portfolio management with published percentage pricing, as well as those with $100,000 who want access to financial advisors.\n\nVersus Empower, Betterment publishes its advisory fees directly on its website, while Empower states that fees are based on assets managed and discloses them in its Form ADV rather than as a public percentage on its website. Both services set a $100,000 minimum for human advisor access. Empower offers free tracking tools for non-clients, while Betterment charges $5 per month on investing accounts under $24,000 that lack recurring deposits.\n\nWorked cost example: On a $100,000 portfolio enrolled in Betterment Premium, the 0.65% annual advisory fee costs $650 per year ($100,000 multiplied by 0.0065). For Empower Personal Strategy, the annual advisory fee is calculated on assets managed per its Form ADV schedule."

Wealthfront also offers a brokerage account for stocks and Exchange-Traded Funds (ETFs), along with cash and borrowing services.\n\nWealthfront fits investors who want automated, software-driven portfolio rebalancing and tax-loss harvesting.\n\nVersus Empower, Wealthfront relies entirely on software algorithms rather than human financial planners to manage portfolios. There is no minimum deposit required to open an account, and investing begins at $10. For accounts with $25,000 or more, Fidelity Go provides financial coaching at a published 0.35% rate, whereas Empower provides dedicated advisor access beginning at $100,000 with fees detailed in its Form ADV.\n\nWorked cost example: On a $20,000 portfolio, Fidelity Go charges $0 in advisory fees. On a $100,000 portfolio, Fidelity Go's 0.35% fee equals $350 per year ($100,000 multiplied by 0.0035) and includes one-on-one coaching sessions.       "heading": "How to Choose a Dave Alternative",
      "body": "Four factors separate these apps: monthly subscription fees, transfer speeds and costs, maximum advance amounts, and account requirements.\n\nStart with the fixed subscription cost. On a $100 advance sent to an external card, MoneyLion Turbo costs $9.00, while a $200 advance costs $18.00. EarnIn charges a flat Lightning Speed fee starting at $3.99, while standard delivery is $0 across 1 to 2 business days.     "intro": "Stash, Betterment, Wealthfront, Fidelity Go, and Schwab Intelligent Portfolios are five Acorns alternatives for automated investing. The right choice depends on whether you prefer a flat monthly subscription, an assets under management (AUM) percentage fee, a zero-fee advisory tier, or a dedicated cash allocation.\n\n[Acorns](https://www.acorns.com/) packages its automated micro-investing features into tiered subscriptions: Bronze costs $4 per month, Silver costs $8 per month, and Gold costs $12 per month. Acorns lists no hidden costs or transaction fees, charging just one monthly subscription payment. Acorns Advisers is an investment adviser registered with the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC), and Acorns Securities provides the brokerage services as a member of the Financial Industry Regulatory Authority (FINRA).\n\nBecause Acorns uses flat dollar subscriptions rather than a percentage fee, small accounts pay a large share of their balance every year.     "chooseSection": {
      "heading": "How to Choose an Acorns Alternative",
      "body": "Selecting an alternative to Acorns comes down to how your account balance interacts with flat monthly fees versus percentage advisory rates. Acorns charges a flat dollar fee ($4, $8, or $12 per month) regardless of whether your investment balance is $50 or $50,000. On small balances, flat fees create high percentage drag. For example, $4 per month equals $48 per year. On a $500 balance, that $48 fee represents 9.6% of your assets annually. On a $2,000 balance, $48 per year represents 2.4% annually. Conversely, on a $50,000 balance, $48 per year is less than 0.10%.\n\nPercentage-based alternatives charge an annual assets under management (AUM) fee. Wealthfront charges 0.25% per year, meaning an account balance of $2,000 costs $5 per year. Betterment charges $5 per month ($60 per year) on balances under $24,000, but drops to 0.25% per year if you establish recurring deposits of $200 per month or cross the $24,000 mark. At $24,000, Betterment's 0.25% annual fee comes to exactly $60 per year.\n\nZero-fee alternatives are a third cost model. Fidelity Go charges a $0 advisory fee under $25,000. At $25,000 it charges 0.35% per year. Schwab Intelligent Portfolios charges no advisory fee or commissions at all. It does require a $5,000 opening deposit, and it keeps part of the portfolio as cash at Charles Schwab Bank, which is insured by the FDIC. Schwab says this cash can pay a lower yield than cash options outside the program.\n\nFour factors drive the comparison:\n\n- Account balance size: Compare the flat monthly fee against an AUM percentage to see which takes fewer dollars out of your portfolio each year.\n- Deposit routines: Determine if you plan to deposit spare change through automated round-ups, schedule fixed recurring deposits, or transfer lump sums.\n- Account types needed: Check whether you need an individual taxable brokerage account, an individual retirement account (IRA) such as a traditional, Roth, or SEP IRA, or a custodial account for children.\n- Cash and banking features: Identify whether you need an integrated checking account with debit card access, fee-free automated teller machines (ATMs), or a separate cash reserve with an annual percentage yield (APY)."
    },
    "alternatives": [
      {
        "heading": "Stash Subscription Plans and Assets Under Management Fees",
        "body": "Stash offers an automated and self-directed investing platform through The Stash Plan, which costs $12 per month, or $108 per year when billed annually (a 25% savings). Stash includes 24/7 financial guidance, a personalized investment plan, Stock-Back rewards worth up to $120 per year, a $10,000 life insurance policy, and a 3% retirement match on contributions worth up to $225 per year. Stash offers a 7-day trial and states no account minimums on its pricing page. It supports taxable brokerage accounts, retirement accounts, and custodial accounts for children. Users can pick individual stocks and exchange-traded funds (ETFs) or use managed diversified portfolios.\n\nIn addition to the subscription, Stash charges an annual AUM fee of 0.25% on Smart Portfolios with balances of $1,000 or more, as well as on Managed Retirement Accounts. This means a customer using a managed portfolio pays both the subscription and the percentage fee.\n\nConsider an investor with a $5,000 balance in a managed portfolio. On Stash, annual subscription billing costs $108 per year, while the 0.25% AUM fee on $5,000 adds $12.50 per year, bringing the total annual cost to $120.50. On monthly billing ($12 per month), the annual total is $144 plus $12.50, which equals $156.50. On Acorns Bronze, that same $5,000 balance costs $48 per year ($4 per month), while Acorns Gold costs $144 per year ($12 per month) with no additional percentage advisory fee.\n\nStash falls short of Acorns Bronze on base pricing, since Stash costs $12 per month compared to $4 per month for Acorns Bronze. Stash also adds a 0.25% advisory fee on top of its subscription for managed accounts over $1,000, whereas Acorns includes portfolio management inside its subscription. Stash fits investors who want to combine self-directed stock picks with automated portfolios, use Stock-Back rewards, and access a bundled life insurance policy."
      },
      {
        "heading": "Betterment Account Fees and Balance Thresholds",
        "body": "Betterment provides automated investing portfolios across individual taxable accounts, traditional IRAs, Roth IRAs, SEP IRAs, Solo 401(k) accounts, and self-directed investing. Betterment Cash Reserve is a high-yield cash account with no fees, no stated balance minimums, and FDIC insurance through program banks. An account with $30,000 pays 0.25% on Betterment, which equals $75 per year. On Acorns Bronze, that same $30,000 account costs $48 per year.       "body": "A Betterment alternative is easiest to compare on two numbers: the invested balance and the monthly deposit. Fidelity describes its margin rates as 'among the most competitive in the industry'.       "heading": "How to Choose a Gusto Alternative",
      "body": "Picking a Gusto alternative starts with the number of people paid each month, because every published price here includes a per-person fee. The same team costs $80 + 10 x $12 = $200 a month ($2,400 a year) on Gusto Plus and $180 + 10 x $22 = $400 a month ($4,800 a year) on Gusto Premium.\n\nAsk five questions of each plan before comparing totals.\n\n- How many people are paid each month, and what do the base fee and the per-person fee add up to?\n- Does the plan file payroll taxes? Patriot Basic Payroll leaves tax filing to the employer, while Patriot Full Service, Square full-service payroll and Gusto Simple list tax filings.\n- Does payroll run in more than one state? Gusto Simple runs basic payroll in one state, multi-state payroll starts on Gusto Plus, and Justworks Payroll also lists multi-state payroll.\n- Are contractors paid? Gusto lists domestic contractor payments on all three plans, Patriot pays contractors in payroll, Square sells contractor-only payroll and Justworks lists contractor payments.\n- Is a PEO wanted? If a quote comes back as a base fee plus a per-person fee, multiply the per-person fee by headcount, add the base fee and set the total beside Gusto Simple's $109 for 10 people."

      "heading": "When Gusto Is Still the Better Fit",
      "body": "Gusto fits businesses that want payroll, benefits and HR tools on one account with no contract and free setup. All three Gusto plans list direct deposits or printable checks, payroll approvals, Payroll on AutoPilot, an employee app, an employer mobile app, multiple pay rates and schedules, domestic contractor payments, and expenses and reimbursements.
